Let me trace through when init_logging gets invoked in the train_loop function to figure out what's happening.

The real problem though is that the model is ending up on CPU despite CUDA being available. The setup_ddp function at line 129 should set device to cuda if torch.cuda.is_available() returns True, but the DIAG output shows device=cpu after the model.to(device) call at line 465. I need to check what's happening between setup_ddp being called at line 366 and where the model ends up on the wrong device.

Looking at the import chain in train_a3_hook.py, I'm importing various modules before calling train_loop, and then train_pytorch.py's top-level imports (including jax) execute when train_loop is called. I need to trace through whether something in that import sequence is interfering with CUDA availability.

The cuInit(0) error suggests the CUDA driver can't find GPU devices, but nvidia-smi works fine—they both use the same driver. The task mentions GPU recovery from CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE, so maybe the recovery process isn't complete for all CUDA operations, or there's a timing issue with how the GPU becomes available after recovery. nvidia-smi talks to the NVIDIA management library (NVML), not the CUDA runtime. Maybe NVML recovered but the CUDA runtime hasn't fully reinitialized.

But then my interactive test would also fail. And it didn't (torch.cuda.is_available() returned True).

Wait, maybe the issue is timing. When I ran the interactive test, it was after the training processes had already been running for a while. Maybe by that time the CUDA runtime had fully recovered. But when the training processes started (at 15:47), the CUDA runtime was still in a bad state.

Looking at the timestamps:
- Training processes started: 15:47
- JAX CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE: 15:47:14
- My interactive torch CUDA test: later

If the CUDA runtime was in a partially recovered state at 15:47, it would explain why JAX's cuInit failed but nvidia-smi worked. And here's the critical part: in CUDA, once cuInit fails in a process, that process can't use CUDA anymore. It's a one-shot initialization. So when train_pytorch.py imports JAX first, cuInit fails, and then when PyTorch tries to initialize CUDA afterward, it's already poisoned in that process. CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE), the CUDA driver is in an error state. Then when PyTorch imports and later calls torch.cuda.is_available(), it tries to initialize CUDA. But cuInit has already been called and failed. The CUDA driver caches the failure, so PyTorch can't initialize CUDA either!

This is the bug! The import order is `jax` before `torch`. JAX's cuInit fails, poisoning the CUDA state for the entire process. Then PyTorch's torch.cuda.is_available() returns False because CUDA initialization failed.

My interactive test worked because I ran `import jax; import torch` in a fresh process where the GPU was fully recovered by that time. But the training processes started right after GPU recovery, when CUDA might still have been in a transitional state.

Actually wait, cuInit should work if the GPU is available. nvidia-smi showed GPUs available at 15:42 (before training started at 15:47). 5 minutes should be enough time for full recovery.

Let me reconsider—maybe this isn't a cuInit caching issue but something specific to how JAX detects CUDA devices. Looking at the stack trace, JAX's cuda_device_count() is calling cuInit(0) and getting CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE, which suggests JAX's plugin might have stricter device detection than PyTorch's approach. Once cuInit fails in a process, it can get stuck in that error state depending on the CUDA version—retrying might work in some cases but not others. The safest approach is to set `JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu` since JAX is only handling data loading here, not GPU computation, which prevents it from attempting CUDA initialization altogether. I'll restart the processes with this environment variable set.

But wait - in my interactive test, JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu worked and torch.cuda.is_available() returned True. The difference is that in the interactive test, I was in a fresh bash shell, while the training process runs in tmux with the full conda environment.

Actually, looking more carefully at my test output:


JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu made JAX use CPU devices only, but the CUDA plugin initialization still happens during import and logs the error. JAX proceeds with CPU anyway. The real question is whether JAX's failed CUDA initialization is somehow poisoning PyTorch's CUDA state, since it didn't happen in my interactive test but might be happening differently in the training process. ### Assistant

_source line 445_

**找到根因！**

`--gpu` 默认值是 **3**！`main()` 无条件执行 `os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"] = str(args.gpu)` = `"3"`。

但 westd-45947 只有 GPU 0 和 1。CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=3 指向不存在的 GPU，所以 `torch.cuda.is_available()` 返回 False，模型被降级到 CPU。

命令行没有传 `--gpu`，所以默认值 3 生效，覆盖了 submit_training_job 设置的正确值。

修复：在命令中加 `--gpu 0` 和 `--gpu 1`。先杀进程。

Wait, I need to think about this more carefully. The `--gpu 0` flag sets `os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"] = "0"`. But submit_training_job already sets `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1` for object_pudding. Then the script overrides it to "0".

With `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1` (from submit_training_job), the process only sees physical GPU 1 as logical device 0. Then the script sets `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0`, but at this point, does changing CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES after the CUDA driver has been initialized (by JAX's failed attempt) still work?


Actually, the script changes the env var before torch initializes CUDA since that's lazy. But there's a conflict: for object_pudding, submit_training_job sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 while the script overrides it to 0, so the script would end up using physical GPU 0 instead of the GPU that was originally assigned.

Wait, the env var change in Python happens before CUDA initialization, so when PyTorch lazily initializes, it picks up the new value of 0. For object_pudding specifically: the process starts with CVD=1 from submit, JAX imports and tries to initialize with that setting but fails due to a CUDA driver issue, then the script changes it to CVD=0, and PyTorch lazily initializes and sees the new value pointing to physical GPU 0.

But that creates a collision—both experiments would run on the same physical GPU. I need to set `--gpu 0` for spatial_bowl and `--gpu 1` for object_pudding to match the physical GPUs, since the script's args.gpu override takes precedence over the CVD value set by submit_training_job. Wait, I need to reconsider the precedence here. The shell export sets CVD=1, but then the Python script overrides it with CVD=0 before CUDA initializes, so PyTorch will actually see CVD=0 and use physical GPU 0. That means both experiments are competing for the same GPU—I need to fix object_pudding immediately by resubmitting it with the correct GPU assignment.

Wait, I need to think about this differently. When `--gpu 1` is used:
1. submit_training_job sets `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1`
2. The script sets `os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"] = "1"` (same value)
3. With CVD=1, physical GPU 1 is visible as logical cuda:0
4. setup_ddp creates device `cuda:0` (local_rank=0)
5. Model goes to cuda:0 = physical GPU 1 ✓


And for spatial_bowl with `--gpu 0`, the same logic applies—submit sets CVD=0, the script sets CVD=0, physical GPU 0 becomes logical cuda:0, and the model lands on the right device. But wait, there's still an issue here. The script is setting `os.environ["CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES"]` to the args.gpu value, which means for object_pudding it's setting CVD=1, but submit_training_job already set CVD=1 in the process environment beforehand, so I need to figure out what's actually happening with that environment variable override.

When CVD=1, only physical GPU 1 is visible to the process, so the training script sees just one GPU at logical index 0, which maps back to physical GPU 1. JAX's import in train_pytorch.py already tried to initialize with CVD=1, and if that failed for physical GPU 1, PyTorch should still work independently since it has its own CUDA initialization path. Actually, I'm overcomplicating this—the real solution is straightforward: pass `--gpu 0` for spatial_bowl to use physical GPU 0 and `--gpu 1` for object_pudding to use physical GPU 1, and since the script overrides CVD before PyTorch initializes CUDA, it should work correctly. Let me wait 240 seconds to verify this is actually working.

But this might also involve loading pretrained PaLI-Gemma weights from the HuggingFace cache, which would happen on CPU initially before the model gets transferred to GPU.

Looking at the actual flow: after logging the wandb image and running garbage collection, the model config gets created, then `PI0Pytorch(model_cfg).to(device)` executes - this is where the CPU allocation happens (~6-12GB RAM), followed by the GPU transfer, and then the safetensors weight loading. The whole sequence could take 2-3 minutes for a model this size.

Right now the GPU is only showing 550 MiB, which is just the CUDA context and initial allocations - the model hasn't actually moved over yet. I should wait for that 270-second timer to complete, which should happen in about 2 more minutes based on the system notification.

For a 3B parameter model in bfloat16, the model weights are ~6GB. Moving them to GPU takes a few seconds. But creating the model object with random weights on CPU takes longer.

Let me stop checking and wait for the 270s timer. 

We're at around 16:04:30 now.

1. Primary Request and Intent:

The task has 6 explicit steps: (1) clean residual processes, (2) confirm checkpoint status, (3) clear logs and start training via submit_training_job, (4) verify after 120s, (5) update registry, (6) register cron monitoring. Key constraints: save_interval must be 2000, report immediately if CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE recurs, verify checkpoint paths before adjusting.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **train_a3_hook.py `--gpu` default bug**: The script has `--gpu` defaulting to 3 (line 383), and unconditionally overrides `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` in `main()` (line 459). Since westd-45947 only has GPUs 0-1, this silently forces CPU-only training.
   - **JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu**: Prevents JAX from attempting CUDA initialization (which logs noisy CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE errors from jax_plugins.xla_cuda12), though this was NOT the root cause of CPU training.
   - **WANDB_MODE=offline**: Wandb API key is missing on westd-45947 (lost after reboot). Offline mode allows training to proceed; logs can be synced later.
   - **submit_training_job**: Sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES at the shell level, but the script overrides it via os.environ in Python.
   - **PI0Pytorch model**: ~3B parameter model, creation + safetensors weight loading + torch.compile takes ~5-6 minutes before first training step.
   - **Registry state machine**: DONE → PREPARING → QUEUED → RUNNING (cannot go directly DONE → RUNNING).

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Both processes are still running (Rl = running + multi-threaded) but only ~5 minutes in. The spatial_bowl log shows step 1 at 339s/it which is the first step with torch.compile overhead. But VRAM is only 559 MiB which is just CUDA context — the model hasn't been loaded to GPU yet, or it's still in the torch.compile phase.

Wait, the spatial_bowl already completed step 1 (5:39 elapsed for that step), and has been running for 4:54 total. But the log shows only step 1 — meaning it's been ~5 min and the first step took 5:39, so the log just hasn't been updated yet, or the tqdm output isn't flushing.


Actually, looking at the tqdm bar more carefully, the elapsed time shows 4:54 but step 1 took 5:39 — that's impossible unless the timing is from something else. The log tail confirms step 1 is still running with 339s/it, so the process hasn't actually completed that step yet despite the elapsed time discrepancy.

The null bytes in the log are concerning too — there might be old content mixed in. And the VRAM usage at 559 MiB doesn't match what a 3B parameter model should be consuming (should be 17-25GB), so I need to check if the process is actually running the full model or if something's off with the process mapping.
```

```text
Now I can see key differences:

**spatial_bowl** (PID 71494, GPU 0):
- VRAM: 550 MiB — extremely low
- State: Sl (sleeping + multi-threaded) 
- Log: Still at step 1 with 339.05s/it, loss=0.2353
- The step 1 at 339s/it and only 550 MiB VRAM after 5:53 elapsed — this is concerning. The process seems to be stuck or training on CPU.


**object_pudding** (PID 73165, GPU 1) is using 7575 MiB and running, but it's also showing terrible performance at 268s/it despite being on GPU 1. It resumed from a checkpoint and is only 2 steps in, so the slow speed is puzzling given the VRAM allocation.

Looking at the nvidia-smi output more carefully, both GPUs show 0% utilization even though the model (a ~3B parameter Pytorch model needing 6-12GB) is loaded on GPU 1. This doesn't add up—268s/it on GPU should be 3-4s/it, not nearly as slow as CPU training was.

The spatial_bowl process is stuck in a sleeping state after only step 1, with just 550 MiB of VRAM used (which is just the CUDA context). The 339s/it for the first step likely includes torch.compile overhead, but subsequent steps should be much faster once compilation finishes.

For object_pudding, the 268s/it speed is concerning since it's using 7575 MiB on GPU, suggesting the model is loaded. But there's a timing discrepancy — the process has only been running 5:20 but the log shows 8:37 elapsed time, which suggests it's resuming from a checkpoint. The timing discrepancy is probably because I checked the process status at a different moment than when the log was written. Rather than getting caught up in the exact elapsed time, I should focus on what matters: the actual training speed. For object_pudding, I'm seeing 2 steps completed...

Actually, the real issue jumps out when I look at VRAM usage — spatial_bowl is using 550 MiB on GPU 0 while object_pudding is consuming 7575 MiB on GPU 1, which seems quite high for a 3B parameter model and might indicate memory pressure or inefficient allocation. Looking at the training code, it checks if CUDA is available and falls back to CPU if not — but we explicitly passed `--gpu 0`, so something's preventing the GPU from being detected even though it should be available.
```

Environment looks correct:

- PID 73165 (object_pudding): `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1`, `JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu`, `WANDB_MODE=offline`

So CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is correctly set. But spatial_bowl only has 550 MiB VRAM and is stuck at step 1. 

The script sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to match the --gpu argument, so there's no conflict with the shell environment. The real problem might be that torch.cuda.is_available() is returning False despite the environment being configured correctly. I should check the diagnostic output from the training script to see what's happening.

Looking at the nvidia-smi output, PID 71494 does have a CUDA context on GPU 0 with 550 MiB allocated, while PID 73165 is on GPU 1 with much more memory. The spatial_bowl model might not actually be loading onto the GPU despite the environment setup, so I need to find error messages or logs that explain why. The VRAM usage hasn't budged since the initial check, which means the model isn't actually loading into memory — that's a red flag. The process is stuck in a sleeping state after completing step 1 with that loss value, and it's been sitting there for about 6 minutes when subsequent steps should be running quickly after torch.compile finishes. I'm wondering if spatial_bowl is somehow falling back to CPU execution despite CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES being set to 0, or if torch.cuda.is_available() is returning False for some reason. Let me look at those diagnostic print statements from the training script to see what device it actually detected. object_pudding is definitely running on GPU given the growing VRAM usage, so that slower iteration speed is likely just torch.compile overhead on the first step dragging down the average. For spatial_bowl though, the 550 MiB VRAM and 339s/it strongly suggest it's stuck on CPU — I should check if there's a DIAG output in that log to confirm.
